A UID without an account whose user is alive at the sign-in service gets "no access", and a UID whose user is gone gets "account gone": an account could not be created for it;
  - a re-sign-in by the window is a new sign-in at the sign-in service, so it gives a new `auth_time` (sources 11.1). How `app` refreshes the sign-in `app` decides; what `api` recommends: compare `authTime` with the `issuedAtTime` of the token just refreshed, not with the device's clock, sign in again by the window on a press, and on `auth/user-mismatch` ask `api`;
  - other devices: "account gone" reaches them while their ID token lives, about an hour after `DeleteUser`. Later the Firebase SDK signs out by itself with `auth/user-token-expired`, and the device does not reach `api` (sources 11.1).

— *Why:* 5 minutes, as in canvas-api (sources 12.1) and as the sample check of the Firebase documentation: `< 5 * 60`, otherwise 401 (sources 11.1). The Connect protocol defines the code `unauthenticated` as "Caller doesn't have valid authentication credentials for the operation", and for "account gone" "if the user can't be identified" fits too (sources 11.3). The code itself tells `app` to sign in again, so a build that does not know the cause does what is needed. RFC 9470 also answers a sign-in that is not fresh enough with 401, with `max_age` (sources 11.2). The class of refusal is needed because `VerifyIDTokenAndCheckRevoked` does not give the token on a refusal and names a deleted user `IsUserNotFound`, which `IsIDTokenInvalid` does not cover (sources 11.1): without an analysis of its own, "account gone" cannot be told from an expired sign-in or from a failure. The fresh sign-in is last so that a repeat of a deletion after a lost answer gets "account gone" or "no profile" without a new sign-in window.

— *Rejected:*
- "fresh sign-in needed" by the code `failed_precondition` or `permission_denied`: a build that does not know the cause would not sign in again by the code;
- "account gone" by the code `not_found`: this is the code of "no profile", and `app` must sign out;
- the PIN instead of a fresh sign-in: the PIN is a barrier of the interface, not a boundary of security;
- a fresh sign-in for the export: the same token reads the profile, the progress and the attempts anyway, and the export with deletion in one step goes through a fresh sign-in anyway;
- 10 minutes — the window for other hands on the device is twice as wide; 2 minutes — an owner who paused to think on the confirmation screen would sign in once more;
- a token the SDK did not accept answered `unavailable`: `app` would repeat a forged token;
- deciding for `app` how it refreshes the sign-in: this is its code, and `api` checks only `auth_time`.

— *Consequence:* `internal/authn/`; `internal/rpc/`; `internal/account/`; `internal/deletion/`; `docs/architecture.md`.

**R21. A profile is deleted separately from the account: by a transaction of the database, then the tuple of OpenFGA. The account by the mark "being deleted" in the same transaction as all its data, then by the tuples, the revocation of tokens and the user at the sign-in service, and last by the account's row; what did not pass the operator's job finishes. Of a child consent a minimal record remains for 5 years, of an adult's consent nothing. The users of the sign-in service without an account of `api` are deleted after 30 days without a sign-in.**
- **What was decided:**
  - a profile is deleted separately from the account, by `can_manage` and a fresh sign-in. The owner profile the same way, and the last profile does not delete the account: the account holds the admission, the tariff and the counters. First one transaction: the account's row `FOR KEY SHARE`, with a check of the mark, the profile's row `FOR UPDATE`, and everything about the profile is deleted — the attempts, the observations, the words of hints, the submissions with a rubric, the stored works, the lesson runs, the evidence log, the cache, the engine choices, the connected modules, the profile's counters, the idempotency keys and the profile itself. The child consent becomes a minimal record, an adult's consent is deleted whole, and the account's counters stay. After `COMMIT`, the tuple of OpenFGA, and the answer "deleted". If the tuple was not deleted, it is "dangling": there is no row of the profile, and a request by it gets "no profile", and the daily job removes it. A repeat at which the tuple is there and the row is not deletes the tuple and answers "no profile", the same refusal as for a profile that is not there;
  - a learner's own consent is withdrawn by the learner themselves: `WithdrawOwnConsent` deletes the profile, as the deletion of a profile does, with the minimal record of consent;
  - the account, by the account from the token and a fresh sign-in:
    1. one transaction. The account's row `FOR UPDATE`, and on it the mark "being deleted" with the time. Everything about each profile, as above. The child consents into minimal records, the adults' consents and the "waiting" consents without a profile whole. The account's counters and the account's fields: the statement, the PIN, the tariff, the offset, the end of the day, the date of the last request. The binding of the invite code is removed, and the code stays redeemed: SHA-256 without a binding is not personal data, and a new account needs a new code. The account's row keeps the ID, the UID of the sign-in service and the time of the mark;
    2. the tuples of all profiles: `api` reads them by `user:<account ID>` and deletes them in batches, and skips the missing ones, as canvas-api does (sources 11.4);
    3. `RevokeRefreshTokens`, then `DeleteUser`; "no such user" means that the step is done already. The step goes even if step 2 did not pass, and it is done when `DeleteUser` passed: if the revocation did not pass, `DeleteUser` goes anyway, since a refresh token does not serve a deleted user (sources 11.1);
    4. only when steps 2 and 3 have passed, a transaction: the cleanup of step 1 once more, for the rows that were written late, and the account's row itself;
    5. the answer "deleted", and `app` erases everything of the account and signs out.

    If steps 2–4 did not pass, the answer is "deleted" anyway: there is nothing about the learners in the database, and the job finishes the rest. A repeat after a lost answer gets "account gone", and `app` does the same;
  - races. Each write transaction under an account takes its row `FOR KEY SHARE` and checks the mark, and the deletion of the account takes it `FOR UPDATE`: a request that passed the checks before the mark creates under a marked account neither a profile, nor a consent, nor a PIN, nor a counter. The lock is `FOR KEY SHARE`, not `FOR SHARE`: a request that then updates the account's row itself — the date of the request, the PIN counter — would with `FOR SHARE` fall into a deadlock with a neighbouring request of the same account, and `FOR KEY SHARE` does not hinder such an update and still conflicts with the `FOR UPDATE` of deletion. The operator's command that confirms a consent rejects a marked account. A row about a profile that is written after its deletion fails on the foreign key, and an `UPDATE` that did not find the row answers "no profile". The quota reserve of a deleted profile is counted only to the account's counter;
  - the operator's job is the daily job of retention periods, the command `api sweep`. It finishes the marks older than the request timeout: steps 2–4. It deletes by period by the same route, only without a fresh sign-in, and checks the period under the lock of the row: a profile after 12 months without activity, an account after 12 months without requests. It deletes the users of the sign-in service without an account of `api` who have not signed in or refreshed the token for 30 days: the iteration is `Users` of the Admin SDK, and the time is `LastLogInTimestamp` and `LastRefreshTimestamp` of its `UserMetadata` (sources 11.1). It cleans the "dangling" tuples and the `changelog` of OpenFGA (sources 11.7), and erases the minimal records of consent whose period has ended. A period that depends on the job is called "until the next successful pass of the job";
  - consent. A minimal record is only for a child consent: the ID of the consent record, the date, the method of the check and the date of deletion, without the account's and the profile's ID, the pseudonym, the age category and the country. It can be linked to the family only through the export file at the owner and the scan of the signed form at the operator. It lives 5 years after the deletion of the account or the profile, and then the daily job erases it. The daily job erases the scan of the form too: of a waiting consent on the day of deletion, of a confirmed one after the same 5 years;
  - what stays after deletion. The problem reports stay until their period, without the account's, the profile's and the attempt's ID. The stored works leave nothing but Neon's history, within its window. The deletion of the user at the sign-in service does not remove the link "Sign in with Google" in the owner's Google account — so it was on the dev project —: `app` is to remove it by revoking the Google token at the deletion of the account, after a fresh sign-in, as this token `app` gets at a sign-in by the window and `api` does not hold it; until `app` does so, the owner removes the link themselves. [architecture.md](architecture.md) names every place where something stays after deletion, and for how long: the database of `api`, the mark, a "dangling" tuple, the `changelog`, the history of Neon, the sign-in service and its users without an account, the logs of `api`, of Cloud Run and of the hosting of `app`, the AI providers, the eval samples, payment, this device and other devices, the export file, the account's counters, the invite code and that link;
  - the export. Of a profile by `can_manage`, of the account by the account from the token, without a fresh sign-in. The content is what [architecture.md](architecture.md) marks as going into the export: for an attempt, the close reason, for a lesson's task, the run ID and the segment place, for a child's profile, "ask before showing", the lesson runs, the submissions with a rubric, the stored works, the counters — the profile's in the profile's export, the account's in the account's export —, the consents whole, the date of the last request. Without secrets and service data: the UID, the PIN hash, its counter and lock, the binding of the code, the seed, the idempotency keys and the tuples. The export is `api.zip`, by the part `export/` of `spec`, and "export and delete in one step" is at `app`: a fresh sign-in, the export, the deletion.

— *Why:* first the database, then the tuples, as canvas-api does (sources 11.4): in the reverse order a failure after the tuples made a repeat impossible. The mark and the job close what was a hole in canvas-api: there only the client led a repeat (sources 11.4), a failure after the database left the Firebase user without a period, and after the revocation of tokens a repeat required a new sign-in. The mark keeps the UID, without which the sign-in service cannot be finished, and answers the other devices "account gone". Step 3 goes even without step 2, because the sign-in service holds the identity data, for up to 180 days after deletion too, and the tuples only IDs. The minimal record keeps the date and the method of the check without the child's data, and its own ID is there so that it can be checked against the signed form at the operator. An adult's consent has no form, there is nothing to check the record against, and keeping it would mean keeping data with no use at all. A user of the sign-in service without an account appears for everyone who signed in without an invite code, and for one who signed in again after deletion: the SDK gives them a new UID. Without the job nobody would delete them, and the 180 days of Firebase begin only with deletion. 30 days are enough to get a code.

— *Rejected:*
- as in canvas-api, without the mark and the job: a failure after the database would leave the user of the sign-in service without a period;
- the sign-in service first: on a failure of the database the owner could not sign in to repeat, and the data would stay without an owner until the period of 12 months;
- deferred deletion with a window to cancel: the data would live longer, and the fresh sign-in protects against an accident anyway;
- deleting the account together with the last profile: the account holds the admission, the tariff and the counters, and a new profile would then require a new code;
- deleting the child consent whole: nothing would show which check the consent passed;
- a minimal record for an adult's consent too: there is nothing to check it against;
- a record with the account's ID: a link to the deleted family without need; a record without an ID of its own: it cannot be checked against the form either;
- keeping the users without an account 12 months, as an account: for a year the Google account of a person whom the platform never admitted would be kept; not deleting them at all: data without a period.

— *Consequence:* `internal/deletion/`; `internal/sweep/`; `internal/store/`; `internal/store/migrations/`; `internal/export/`; `internal/authn/`; `internal/authz/`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## Result of the detailing

**R22. [architecture.md](architecture.md) describes the service as it is built: its design holds each part with what it rests on, and a summary of the service as a whole names the parts, where each is decided, what each needs from outside and whether it is in the first app. What is still to decide keeps its place in the list of what is to decide, with when to come back. When a decision changes, every place of the design that says it changes with it.**
- **What was decided:**
  - the list of what is to decide keeps each item in its place: a decided one says so and where the design holds it, and one deliberately deferred names when to come back;
  - the summary of the service as a whole is no new design: the parts of the service, where each is decided, what it needs from outside and whether it is in the first app, and the order in which the code is built;
  - a change of a decision is carried into every part of the design that speaks of it, not only the part it concerns: a builder who reads two parts finds one rule in both;
  - what of the decisions changes the card of `api` or another place of `spec` goes as a row «В реестр spec» of table 3;
  - the code came in two epics: first the skeleton, the database, sign-in and deletion, which need only the author's dev projects, then the first app, which waits for the libraries; the Connect handlers came with the contract of `spec`, not by a draft of it.

— *Why:* the design was written part by part, each by its own question, and none described the service as a whole; and a change that reached one part and not another would leave the builder two rules. The skeleton, the database, sign-in and permissions are needed by the first app in any case, and deletion goes with them, while almost half of the service waits for nothing that the libraries bring.

— *Rejected:*
- renumbering the list of what is to decide or removing its decided items: the plan and its archives cite them by their places;
- keeping what is known about a part beside its design: it would repeat what the design rests on;
- one epic of code after everything the first app waits for: the skeleton, the database, sign-in and deletion would wait for libraries they do not need;
- the Connect handlers by a draft of the contract: the service stubs are born in `spec`, and `api` would write its own anew.

— *Consequence:* `docs/architecture.md`; `run/QUESTIONS.md`; `run/EPICS.md`.

## The `spec` tag: v0.9.4

**R23. Where the contract `app` ↔ `api` of `spec` decided otherwise than [architecture.md](architecture.md) recommended, the contract is right: [architecture.md](architecture.md) keeps its list of the requests at the level of meaning and names beside it what the contract decided and how it differs. A move to a tag of `spec` is one task: the submodule, the rows of table 3 that the tag answers and the reconciliation with what it brings go together.**
- **What was decided:**
  - the differences are named as a list, not written into the list of the requests: that list says what the requests mean, and the contract what they are on the wire;
  - one move raises the submodule, the Go modules of `spec` and the lines that name the tag in CLAUDE.md and RUN.md, and carries the answers of `spec` into the documents in words;
  - a row that the tag answers only in part stays in table 3, with what is still to decide; and what `spec` leaves to `api` — where a value lies, what a number is — the move does not decide: it goes to the author as a row of table 3.

— *Why:* the contract is the one home of the messages and the fields, and the list of the requests is what it was written by. A move in parts would leave the documents standing on two tags at once between them.

— *Rejected:*
- the move as several tasks — the submodule, the closing of the rows, the reconciliation with the contract: between them the documents would stand on two tags;
- rewriting the list of the requests for the contract: there would be a second source of truth beside it;
- closing a row whose answer the tag gave only in part, or closing a row because `spec` named a value without saying where it lies: what `spec` left to `api` would vanish from the register.

— *Consequence:* `spec/`; `go.mod`; `CLAUDE.md`; `RUN.md`; `run/QUESTIONS.md`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## The `spec` tag: v0.9.11

**R24. `api` moves to a tag of `spec` before the code that rests on what the tag brings: code is built by the decisions of the tag `api` stands on, not of one it waits for. A version of `learner-model` that breaks the API of `engine` `api` takes only together with the version of `engine` built for it.**
- **What was decided:**
  - what a tag decides for `api` — what a record holds, what the image holds, what a library gets as its input — the move takes into the documents in the same task, and the code that changes by it is a task of its own, after the move;
  - `learner-model` and `engine` are raised together where a new version of `learner-model` breaks the API of `engine`.

— *Why:* code written by what `spec` has already changed would be written twice. A pair of libraries at versions not built for each other would not build, or would compute what neither was checked on.

— *Rejected:*
- building the code by what a tag will bring, before it is out: the decision may still change, and the code would follow a draft;
- raising `learner-model` alone: `engine` would get a model whose API it was not built for.

— *Consequence:* `spec/`; `go.mod`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## Deployment facts and the admission rung

**R25. The deployment facts for the AI admission function and the lowest rung of the models' admission rule are held by `infra`, beside the rollout list, and the deployment passes them to `api` by the variables `API_ADMISSION_FACTS_FILE` and `API_ADMISSION_RUNG`; they enter neither the image nor the git of `api`. For the first app, a local file of the author outside the repository, with the rung `author`.**
- **What was decided:**
  - the facts file is the one that the admission table of `spec` names: a fact at a provider for the categories the operator named. Its type and how to read it are `tutor`'s, `table.ReadFacts`. It lies in the repository `infra`, beside the rollout list, and a pull request of `infra` changes it. A deployment states a fact only when it holds, and `minor_safeguards` only when the provider's list of measures is fulfilled and the author decided by analysis that they are enough;
  - the lowest rung that `api` passes to `Table.Select` is `author` or `full`, a value of the same place. `author` only where only the author learns: the first app and the deployment `novatrail` while only the author learns there; any other deployment passes `full`;
  - the deployment of `infra` passes both values to the Cloud Run revision, and `api` reads them at the start of `serve` and by the command `api check`, which the deployment calls before the migrations. The path to the file is absolute and outside the git working tree, as for a secret file. There are no secrets in them;
  - no facts file: no fact holds, and platform AI is closed, while an adult's own key is open; a fact unknown to the vocabulary is an error at start. No value of the rung: `full`, so it is closed by default, as the admission function of `tutor` is. What is missing the row `startup` says by the fields `facts_file` and `rung_set`, without providers, facts and paths;
  - self-hosting holds its own facts and its own rung in its own deployment;
  - the first app is a local file of the author outside the repository, not in the folder `/workspaces/api`, like the key of the Firebase service account: in it the facts, if the table opens with them the cell of platform AI for `adult`, and the rung `author`. Where only the author learns — the first app and `novatrail` while so — the author states `minimal_retention` for `adult` too, although their organization at Anthropic keeps data 30 days, without zero data retention: only the author, an adult, learns, Anthropic keeps only their own work, and the author does not ask for zero data retention. Any other deployment states a fact only when it holds.

— *Why:*
- the facts and the rung are a property of the deployment, not of the build: the operator states them about their contracts and about who learns, and the image of `api` is one for all, with only the binary and the module packages in it;
- self-hosting writes its own facts and does not edit `api` for that: `tutor` designed the file so — in git, by pull request, not built into the image;
- `modules.json` is another matter: the packages are built into the image at the build, and this is a property of the release of `api`;
- removing a fact means closing at once the cells that it opened: the deployment of `infra` does this without a release of `api`;
- `author` where only the author learns: while the admitted models stand at the rung `author`, AI reads only the author's work.

— *Rejected:*
- in `api`, beside `modules.json`: the platform's facts would become the facts of everyone who builds `api`, and a change of a fact would wait for a release of `api`;
- deciding with the first deployment: the admission code reads the values at start, and it needs to know from where;
- `author` refused on every deployment: the deployment where only the author learns would lose the models of that rung.

— *Consequence:* `internal/config/`; `internal/admission/`; `cmd/api/`; `docs/build.md`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## Skeleton

**R26. The skeleton of `api` is a Go service after the model of canvas-api: one binary with commands, configuration from the environment, logs by `log/slog` in JSON under a table of events, errors and panics in the log by kind, and the version is set by the linker. The differences from the model are recorded here, and a reconciliation with the model does not roll them back.**
- **The model** is canvas-api at the commit `0901af0` of its `main`, cloned into a temporary folder; its skeleton is `internal/` from standalone.
- **What was taken:**
  - the configuration, `internal/config`: only the environment, only the declared names, exactly by case, and an empty value is not set. A refusal wraps `ErrInvalid` and names the variable. The port in canonical digits up to 65535. `PORT=0` outside a deployment asks the kernel for a free port, and the row `startup` names it: so the tests of `serve` do not share a port with the tests beside them and with the Postgres of `embedded-postgres`, which takes a free one too, while a free port found in advance another could take first; in a deployment, where the platform names the port, `0` is a refusal. The shutdown time is split: ¾ for draining the requests, ¼ for closing, and in a deployment, where `K_SERVICE` is set, it is shorter than 10 s — the pause of Cloud Run before stopping an instance;
  - start and shutdown, `cmd/api` and `internal/server`: `signal.NotifyContext` on SIGINT and SIGTERM, and a second signal ends the process at once; the port is taken before the row `startup`; an error is written once (`errLogged`); a shutdown during the start is not a failure; a drain that did not fit is a warning `stopped` with `cut_short`;
  - a panic and an error in the log by kind, not by text: a runtime failure in the runtime's words, the rest by type, as `PanicValue` and `errorsField` of canvas-api;
  - the version is `Version`, `Commit` and `Date` of the package `internal/version`, which `-ldflags -X` sets, and tests that the linker sets them and that the build names this path;
  - the health check `GET /health`, without `z` at the end, as in canvas-api.
- **How it differs from the model:**
  - the logs are `log/slog` of the standard library, not zap; the keys of Cloud Logging are the same — `severity`, `message`, `time`;
  - the ban on direct identifiers, IP addresses and content is held by the table of events, `internal/logging/events.go`. The message of a row is the name of the event, the event names its fields, and a field has a rule: a number, a flag, a duration, a word of a closed set, a string in the form of the build — a version, a commit, a date —, or the kind of an error and of a panic, which only the package itself makes. There is no free text. The guard handler writes only this: a field that the event does not name, or a value not by the rule, is dropped, and the row says how many were dropped — `dropped_fields`; an unknown event loses its message and is written as `unregistered_event`; groups are dropped. A new event or field comes with the code that writes it, as a row of the table, and its test holds each row of the rules. The libraries in the process get the same `*slog.Logger`, and their rows pass the same filter. canvas-api has no such design — the linter's bans on `zap.Any` and `zap.Reflect` and the closed sets of the method and the route —, and its table is only declared;
  - one binary with commands — `serve`, `version`, `migrate`, `check`, `after`, `sweep`, `packages` and the operator's commands —, because there is no shell in the image. canvas-api has one command, and its other programs are separate binaries;
  - the standard library instead of viper, gin and errgroup: the configuration reads only the environment, and the Connect handler is an `http.Handler` for `http.ServeMux`;
  - the prefix of the variables is `API_`, and `PORT` and `K_SERVICE` are names of Cloud Run. There are no variables for the environment's name or the log format, like `CANVAS_ENV` and `CANVAS_LOG_FORMAT`: the log is always in JSON, and a deployment is recognized by `K_SERVICE`;
  - a secret is the type `Secret`, which prints as `[redacted]` both in `fmt` with any verb and in the log. The value is from the variable `X` or from a file by an absolute path in `X_FILE`. Both at once is a refusal, and a file in the git working tree too: a key in it is one commit away from the repository. canvas-api has no secrets;
  - the error format `{code, message}` of the package `apierror` is not taken: the refusals of `api` are Connect errors with `Refusal`. An unknown path is 404 without a body, and so is a path that the mux would redirect, to its clean form or to the root of a subtree with its slash: `Routes` asks `mux.Handler`, and a handler of the type of `http.RedirectHandler` is answered 404 without a body (sources 10.6). Such roots are `/novatrail.api.v1.AccountService`, since Connect mounts a subtree for each service, and `/packages/<name>/device`, of the pattern `{path...}`. No handler of `api` answers by a redirect: it would repeat the path and the query in `Location`. One 404 has a body: a request for no method of a service that is not a POST of Connect's protocol gets Connect's own `http.NotFound`, the fixed text "404 page not found", which repeats nothing of the request (sources 10.6). A panic is 500 without a body;
  - the recipes of the checks — `ci-lint` (gofmt and `go vet`), `ci-test` (`-race` and the profile `coverage/go.out`), `ci-vuln` (govulncheck) and `ci-licenses` (go-licenses), at the versions of the `justfile` — by the agreement of the platform's CI, without golangci-lint. In canvas-api `ci-lint` is golangci-lint, and the profile is `coverage.out`. `ci-licenses` takes the list of licenses from `ALLOWED_LICENSES`, which the shared `go.yml` gives, and without it from `just -f spec/justfile licenses-allowed`;
  - `build` builds with `CGO_ENABLED=0` and `-trimpath`, as a production image requires. In canvas-api `CGO_ENABLED=0` is set only by the `Dockerfile` and the release recipe;
  - Go is the devcontainer's: `go.mod` names its version, without a line `toolchain`.
- **The modules of `spec`.** `github.com/altnovatrail/spec` and `github.com/altnovatrail/spec/contracts` come from the Go proxy, without `replace`, at the tag of the submodule. The test `internal/version` imports both and checks by `debug.ReadBuildInfo` that they have one tag number; it does not read the submodule `spec/`: the shared `go.yml` does not take it. Both enter the production binary. The root module comes through `module-kit`, by the package `types/formatv0`, and through the admission of `tutor`, whose admission table is `spec.Admission`: so the root package `spec` and with it `spec.Format` enter it too, as `spec` embeds `format/`, `admission/` and `deployment/` by one `embed.FS`. The libraries that require `spec` — `module-kit`, `tutor` — go into `THIRD_PARTY` and into the `ignore` of Dependabot.

— *Why:*
- the platform carries the skeleton of canvas-api over, and differences without a record the next reconciliation would roll back;
- the table holds the rule of the logs not by the memory of the author of each row but by one place, which has a test: without the guard the log test fails on a field with an email address and a UID;
- the guard stands under `*slog.Logger`, not over it, so the rows of the libraries pass the filter too.

— *Rejected:*
- a closed list of fields without events: any event would carry any allowed field;
- a function for each event instead of `*slog.Logger`: stricter, but the libraries that expect `*slog.Logger` would bypass the filter, and each event would become a new method;
- viper, gin and cobra: dependencies for the sake of what the standard library gives;
- golangci-lint: the license is GPL-3.0, it would enter only as a named exception, and `spec` rejected it for itself. `api` can come back to it by a decision of its own, if `go vet` is not enough;
- the prefix `NOVATRAIL_API_`: unambiguous, but long, and the configuration reads only the declared names anyway;
- a redirect of a path to its clean form: the answer would repeat the path and the query.

— *Consequence:* `cmd/api/`; `internal/config/`; `internal/server/`; `internal/logging/`; `internal/version/`; `justfile`; `go.mod`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## Go CI and coverage

**R27. Go code is checked by the shared `go.yml` of `.github` — "Go / lint", "Go / test", "Go / vuln" and "Go / licenses" —, its CodeQL reads Go too, and coverage goes to Codecov by OIDC. The modules of `spec` and of the contracts are held at the tag of the submodule by `ci-lint`: it checks the versions that the build chooses against the commit of the submodule. The merge is held by these checks and `codecov/patch`. The readers of foreign input have fuzz tests of Go, whose corpus `ci-test` runs, and a task that changes a reader fuzzes it.**
- **The calls of `.github`:**
  - every call — secrets, DCO, documents, "Go", CodeQL, Scorecard and the release — is at one release of `.github`, by SHA with the version in a comment, as `.github/workflows/` pins it, and a move to a release takes it once `gh release verify` has accepted it. Dependabot `github-actions` raises them as one group, `shared-workflows`, `patterns: ["altnovatrail/.github/*"]`: the template of `.github` asks every caller to have it. A move reads what changed in the shared workflows, `git diff` of `.github/workflows` between the releases, and holds the triggers, inputs and permissions of the workflows `api` calls, so the jobs keep theirs and the checks of the ruleset their names;
  - the job "Go" gives `contents: read` and `id-token: write`: the nested coverage job asks for an OIDC token, and a called workflow only lowers permissions. There is no input `same-bits`: `api` computes nothing where identical bits on all builds matter, so neither "Go / targets" nor "Go / arm64" runs;
  - "Go" builds the image of the devcontainer's stages in its job `toolchain` — "Go / toolchain" — only on a miss of the cache of GitHub Actions, keeps it there by the key of the runner's OS and architecture and the hash of `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, and hands it to the jobs after it by an artifact of the run; only a push to `main` saves, and a pull request reads the entries of `main`. The Dockerfile has the stage `toolchain-core`, and its `toolchain` adds nss_wrapper over it, so "Go / lint", "Go / vuln", "Go / licenses" and "Documents" run in the core, and "Go / test" in `toolchain`. A pull request that changes the Dockerfile misses both entries until a push to `main` saves them, and "Go / toolchain" builds both stages once. An entry the cache no longer has costs a build of the stage, not a check;
  - the Go caches: the jobs of "Go" take `GOMODCACHE`, `GOCACHE` and the linter's cache from one folder, by a key a check — the check, the hash of the Dockerfile and of `**/go.sum` —, check what came by `go mod verify`, and on a miss of the exact key restore by its prefix without the hash of `go.sum`, keeping the builds and dropping the modules; only a push to `main` saves. The recipes `ci-lint`, `ci-test`, `ci-vuln` and `ci-licenses` set none of the three, so they run the same with a cache and without one;
  - CodeQL — the input `languages: go`. It takes the archive of Go by the `ARG`s `GO_VERSION` and `GO_SHA256_AMD64` of the stage `toolchain`, checked by the sum, with no image, and there is no Makefile or `build.sh`, which autobuild would run first, in the root of `api`;
  - Dependabot — the ecosystem `gomod`, with a pause of three days, and in `ignore` by name the modules `spec` and `spec/contracts` and the libraries that require `spec` — `module-kit`, `tutor` —: a new patch of such a library may require a `spec` newer than the submodule, and `api` takes it by a task of its own.
- **The own jobs "Image", "Modules" and "Withdrawals"** build their stage by `docker build` through `.github/toolchain.sh`, and read the image from the cache, never saving it, by a step `actions/cache/restore` before the script, by the path and the key that "Go" saves on `main`: "Image" and "Withdrawals" the core — `${{ runner.temp }}/novatrail-toolchain-core.tar` by the key `toolchain-core-<OS>-<architecture>-<hash of the Dockerfile>` —, "Modules" `toolchain` — `${{ runner.temp }}/novatrail-toolchain.tar` —, since its test of the first app's path starts PostgreSQL, which under the runner's uid needs nss_wrapper. `toolchain.sh` loads the file `TOOLCHAIN_TAR` names, deletes it, and builds the stage `TOOLCHAIN_STAGE` names — unset, `toolchain`; another value than the two stops it — from the Dockerfile when there is no file or it does not load as the image `novatrail-toolchain`, the name every workflow of `.github` keeps it under. The stage is the script's, not the workflow's, so a script run by hand builds the stage its check needs. A pull request that changes the Dockerfile builds the stage in these jobs until "Go" on `main` saves its entry. They take no action of `.github` and no Go cache.
- **The modules of `spec` at the tag of the submodule** — after the model of `ci-lint` of module-kit. The commit of the submodule is taken from the git index, so the check goes in CI too, where there is no submodule. The versions of both modules are the ones that the build chooses, `go list -m`, and their commit is `Origin.Hash`, which the Go proxy gives. All three commits must match, and the versions be one. A library that requires a `spec` newer than the submodule — `tutor`, `module-kit` — will raise it by minimal version selection, and "Go / lint" will turn red. It is fixed not by an edit of `go.mod` but by a move to the tag in a separate task. The test `internal/version` holds both modules in `go.mod`.
- **Coverage.** `.github/codecov.yml` is the text of the template of `.github`: changed lines no less than 90%, `codecov/project` only reports. There is no `after_n_builds`: `api` has one upload. The Codecov app is installed at the organization for all repositories, and there is no secret.
- **The ruleset of `main`.** "Go / lint", "Go / test", "Go / vuln", "Go / licenses" and "CodeQL / analyze (go)", with `integration_id` 15368, and `codecov/patch`, with `integration_id` 254, which goes in only once it has arrived: a check required before its first report holds every merge without end. "Go / coverage" and "Go / toolchain" do not go into the ruleset.
- **SonarCloud** reads the Go tests — `*_test.go` and the programs under `testdata/` — as tests, not as code: `sonar.tests` and `sonar.test.inclusions`, as in `spec`, and the submodule `spec/` is excluded both from the code and from the tests. The template of `.github` gives only `sonar.exclusions=spec/**`.
- **README** — the badges of the Go version, of coverage and of OpenSSF Best Practices. `api` has its project on bestpractices.dev at the level passing; the answers to its criteria lie in `.bestpractices.json` in the root, which the site reads from `main` (sources 13.2). `crypto_password_storage` and `crypto_random` are Met: the PIN is stored as a hash, and the salt, the invite codes and the consent request codes are given by `crypto/rand`; `dynamic_analysis` is Met by the fuzz tests. Scorecard's `Fuzzing` and `CII-Best-Practices` read the fuzz functions and the project (sources 13.1, 13.2).
- **The fuzz tests.** Each checks that its reader does not panic, and what holds of what it accepts:
  - `FuzzRead` of `internal/invite` — the code a person types: written in the other case, with O for 0 and I or L for 1, or with dashes and white space between its symbols, it reads to the same hash, and it has 16 symbols; dashes and white space never change whether a text is read;
  - `FuzzFromName` of `internal/packages` — the package's name in the path of a file of a device part: a digest whose `Name` is that name;
  - `FuzzUnpack` of `internal/packages` — the archive of a package from a release, through `packages.Unpack` of `export_test.go`, since the seeds come from `packagestest`, which imports the package: regular files alone, of the package's parts, by clean paths, of no more bytes than the tar holds, and an archive with a link or a sparse entry refused (sources 4.5). The fuzzer varies the tar, and the test gzips it: the gzip is the standard library's, the rules are `api`'s;
  - `FuzzCursorOf` of `internal/learning` — the page token of `GetProgress`, which `app` gives back: a cursor whose token reads back to it, between 1970 and the year 9999;
  - `FuzzRoutes` of `internal/server` — the path and the query of any request, on the mount of `serve`, `rpc.Mount` of every service and the pattern of the files: no answer is a redirect, and a 404 has no body but the fixed text of Connect;
  - `FuzzParseConfig` of `internal/packages` — `modules.json`, which a pull request may change and `cmd/modules-check` reads: a release and a digest of their form, which read back from themselves;
  - `FuzzWorkbookWork` of `internal/learning` — the work of a workbook page that `SubmitWork` checks, `boxesTaken`: whatever `WorkbookWork` it takes, every box is of a box of the page, by its places, none twice, no more than 20, its crop the PNG sent, whole and no larger than 512 px a side;
  - `FuzzWorkExported` of `internal/learning` — the form of a stored work that `PutWork` checks, on the file of a work that `app` writes.

  No fuzz test is given to the rest. The body of a request is parsed by Connect and protobuf, and its fields are then checked in one pass, without decoding: `pin.Valid`, `countries.Valid`, `idem.ValidKey`, `store.CanonicalID` — `uuid.Parse` and its own writing back —; the path of a file of a device part is a key of a map. The operator's files and environment — a secret of `_FILE`, read whole and stripped of one newline, the service account's key, read by `encoding/json` and the SDK, the deployment's facts, read by `table.ReadFacts` of `tutor`, the durations and numbers of `config` by `strconv` and `time` — come from the operator, not a stranger, and their readers are the standard library's and the libraries'. `proto.Unmarshal` of records reads what `api` wrote.
- **The corpus and the run.** The seeds are `f.Add` and `testdata/fuzz/<test>/` of the package, and `go test` runs them without fuzzing, so `ci-test` runs them on every pull request (sources 13.1). The recipe `just fuzz [fuzztime] [test]`, 1m by default, fuzzes each test in turn, or the one named: Go fuzzes one test of a package at a time. It minimizes a new input for 2 s, not 60: the minimizing stops the fuzzing (measurements 5). An input that fails is written to `testdata/fuzz/`, stays in the corpus and fails `ci-test` until the code is fixed. Fuzzing runs in a task that changes a reader (`.claude/rules/go.md`), not in CI and not by a schedule: it is not deterministic, and a pull request would fail by a finding it did not make.

— *Why:*
- the check in `ci-lint` goes in CI on every pull request, without the submodule, and catches both a forgotten side of a move to a tag and a library that raised `spec`;
- `codecov/patch` in the ruleset before it arrived holds any merge;
- the cache in the own jobs: the stage is built in every job of `api`, and the step of the Ubuntu snapshot alone took 81 s and 104 s in two runs of five against 6–9 s in the others (measurements 3.1 spec); a restore and a load take seconds (measurements 9, 10). Reading the entry of "Go" costs one step a job and a script of 20 lines, and the image from the cache is the one the Dockerfile builds, as it pins everything it installs;
- no fuzzing by a schedule: the readers change seldom, fuzzing found no crash the seeds had not shown, and Scorecard's `Fuzzing` scores the fuzz functions themselves (sources 13.1).

— *Rejected:*
- the check by a Go test, as `TestSpecVersion` of tutor: there is no submodule in CI, and the test would check it only on the author's machine;
- relying on the review of a move to a tag: a move that forgot one of the sides would pass green;
- `same-bits`: `api` computes nothing where identical bits on all builds matter;
- `after_n_builds: 2`, as in `spec`: with one upload Codecov would wait for a second without end;
- `codecov/patch` in the ruleset at once: see "Why";
- saving the image from "Image" or "Modules": a second entry of the same file under the same key, which the cache would refuse beside the one of "Go", and a pull request saves none;
- "Modules" in the core: its test of the first app's path begins attempts against PostgreSQL, and the core has no nss_wrapper; a job of its own for that test — a third load of an image for one test;
- the Go caches in the own jobs: the release gives a caller's own jobs no cache, its entries are `go.yml`'s by keys of its own checks;
- a weekly fuzzing job, `just fuzz 5m`, about 40 minutes of the free runner of a public repository (sources 4.4), a finding being a failed job: come back to it when there are more readers or they change more often; ClusterFuzzLite — a new tool, which would need a decision of its own.

— *Consequence:* `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `.github/workflows/codeql.yml`; `.github/workflows/scorecard.yml`; `.github/workflows/withdrawals.yml`; `.github/dependabot.yml`; `.github/codecov.yml`; `.github/toolchain.sh`; `.github/image.sh`; `.github/modules.sh`; `justfile`; `.sonarcloud.properties`; `.bestpractices.json`; `README.md`; `internal/version/`; `.claude/rules/go.md`.

## Release

**R28. A release of `api` comes out by itself, by the shared `release.yml` of `.github`, after green CI on `main`: the static binaries `api_<version>_linux_amd64` and `api_<version>_linux_arm64`, the `Dockerfile` by which the deployment builds the image, the licenses the binaries come with, and `SHA256SUMS`, each file with its signature. The binary takes its commit and that commit's time from a file that `git archive` writes, so the build is reproducible. CI's job "Image" checks the `Dockerfile`: it builds the image the same way as the deployment does, and from the source code, and holds the binary of the image from the source to the bytes of the release's.**
- **How a release comes out:**
  - `.github/workflows/release.yml` is the template of `.github`, at the release of `.github` the other calls are at. `RELEASE_LINE` is set in it, the number is the next patch of the line, and a release comes out only when `paths` change: the list of the kind `go` without `testdata/`, and with it `Dockerfile`, `.gitattributes`, `internal/version/archive.txt` and `modules.json` — what whoever deploys receives;
  - the shared `release.yml` sets the tag itself: a tag set by hand, with no release behind it, stops every following run;
  - the recipe `release-files <version>` writes the files into `dist/`, in the stage `toolchain-core` of the commit being released, which the release builds from the Dockerfile with no cache, on the tree of `git archive` without `.git`, submodules and the token. The shared `release.yml` signs each with cosign without keys, `<file>.sigstore.json`, and checks the signatures and the published release before it stands. `just -f spec/justfile release-verify api <tag> <file>` accepts the files;
  - the condition of the signature is immutable releases and the tag ruleset "v-tags", `update`, `deletion` and `non_fast_forward` with no bypass, set before the first release;
  - the release's README is [build.md](build.md) at the release's tag: it names the files, their check and the commands of a rollout, which the deployment of `infra` runs as jobs of the same image — `api check`, `api migrate` and `api after`, and `api sweep` on a schedule.
- **What is in the release:**
  - binaries for `linux/amd64` and `linux/arm64` with `CGO_ENABLED=0`, `-trimpath`, `-buildvcs=false` and `-s -w`, as canvas-api and module-kit build theirs. There are no other OSes: `api` is a service, and it runs in an image on Linux;
  - the `Dockerfile` — the same as in the repository;
  - `LICENSE`, `LICENSE-go` — the notice of Go's standard library — and the licenses of the modules the binary is built from, by the list `THIRD_PARTY` of the `justfile`, as module-kit has: `release-licenses`, which `ci-lint` calls too, fails on the first module that enters the binary without its license;
  - `SHA256SUMS` over all the files.
- **The commit and the time.** `.gitattributes` gives `export-subst` to the file `internal/version/archive.txt`, `$Format:%H %ct$`, and `git archive` writes the full commit and its time into it. `release-files` passes them to the linker, the time in UTC RFC 3339, and refuses without a file that `git archive` filled in. The binary's date is the commit's time, not the build's: two builds of one commit give the same bytes, and the job "Image" checks this.
- **The `Dockerfile`**, after canvas-api:
  - `build` is `golang` by digest, with the Go of `go.mod`; `runtime` is `gcr.io/distroless/static-debian13:nonroot` by digest, uid 65532, `ENTRYPOINT ["/api"]` and `CMD ["serve"]`, so the same image runs the operator's commands too;
  - `release-packages` and `source-packages` each put a binary into the runtime — from the context `binary` or from the stage `build` — and call it with the command `packages` in exec form, into `/tmp/packages`; the stages `release` and the last one copy the folder to `/packages` with root as owner;
  - `release` is the runtime around the release's binary from the build context `binary`, the file `api`: the deployment builds it, `docker build --target release --build-context binary=<folder>`. It reads no main context, and the build needs the network. The last stage is the same image from the source code, for the checks;
  - the test of `internal/version` holds the tag of `golang` equal to the version of `go.mod`, both images by digest, and the `-X` path the same in the `Dockerfile`, `build` and `release-files`;
  - Dependabot `docker` raises the digests of the root `Dockerfile`, but not the version of `golang`: Go is raised by hand, together with the devcontainer and `go.mod`.
- **The job "Image"** on a runner that has Docker is `.github/image.sh`: `release-files` once from `git archive`, in the stage `toolchain-core`, as the release builds; then the image `--target release` around the `linux/amd64` binary and the image from the source code. `image.sh` takes `/api` out of the image from the source by `docker create` and `docker cp` and `cmp`s it with the release's `linux/amd64` binary: two builds in two stages — `golang` by the stage `build` of the `Dockerfile` and the devcontainer's Go in `release-files`, each with its own module and build caches — give the same bytes. Each image has `version` with the version and the commit, user 65532, `/health`, exit 0 on SIGTERM and the packages of the pinned release, and the job prints their size. The check is required in the ruleset of `main`.

— *Why:*
- a release on each change of the code: the deployment of `infra` builds by a release, the check `release-verify` needs one, and whoever deploys gets the binary that CI checked;
- an image built the same way as the deployment will build it catches an error of the `Dockerfile` before the deployment, not during it;
- the line `startup` and the command `version` name the commit in the binary, and the commit's date keeps the build reproducible;
- the `cmp` with the image's binary holds two builds to the same bytes with no build of its own, as the image from the source is built anyway; a second run of `release-files` was as cold as the first, 169 s of the job's 418, and held two builds in one stage only (measurements 3.1 spec; measurements 9).

— *Rejected:*
- the tag of a release by hand: the shared `release.yml` does not accept such a tag;
- `publish: false` until the first deployment: the check `release-verify` would not take place, and `infra` would have nothing to try;
- Commit and Date "unknown" in the release: the binary would not name its commit;
- only the build of `infra`: an error of the `Dockerfile` would be found at the deployment;
- hadolint and an image scanner: the image is not published, and the base is distroless, whose signature can be verified;
- publishing the image: NovaTrail publishes no image;
- a second run of `release-files` beside the `cmp`: the 169 s it costs;
- comparing the `linux/arm64` binary too: a cross-build in the stage `build` of the `Dockerfile`, a file of production, or a third build, about 86 s, for the same flags.

— *Consequence:* `.github/workflows/release.yml`; `.github/workflows/ci.yml`; `.github/image.sh`; `Dockerfile`; `.gitattributes`; `internal/version/`; `justfile`; `.github/dependabot.yml`; `docs/build.md`.

## Database and migrations

**R29. `api` goes to Neon with the driver pgx v5: the requests of `serve` through Neon's pooler, and the migrations directly, by goose, by the command `api migrate`, embedded in the binary and forward only, by the rule "add first, remove later", which a test holds. The connection strings are secrets outside the repository, both with `sslmode=verify-full`. The tests with the database are `embedded-postgres`, Postgres 17: one server per package and a database of its own for each test. A write under an account goes in a transaction that holds its row `FOR KEY SHARE` and checks the mark "being deleted".**
- **The driver** is pgx, MIT, at the version of `go.mod`, and the pool is `pgxpool`. Not below v5.12.0: in v5.9.0–v5.11.0 `channel_binding=require` was bypassed without `sslmode=verify-full` (sources 3.5).
- **The pooler and the direct connection.**
  - Neon's pooler is PgBouncer in transaction mode: it supports the protocol's prepared statements, and those are what pgx uses in its default mode. It does not support `SET`, `LISTEN`, SQL `PREPARE` and session locks (sources 3.1). A request of `api` is in one transaction, and row locks — `FOR KEY SHARE`, `FOR UPDATE` — live in it, so `serve` goes through the pooler: otherwise the instances of Cloud Run would run short of direct connections.
  - The migrations go directly: goose holds the session lock `pg_try_advisory_lock`, so that two runs do not go at once (sources 3.5), and Neon names migrations work for a direct connection. OpenFGA goes to its database the same way: the requests by a pgx pool in the default mode, the migrations by goose (sources 3.5).
  - So there are two strings, and both are secrets of the configuration: `API_MIGRATE_DATABASE_URL`, the direct one, and `API_DATABASE_URL`, the pooler's. The configuration takes the string from the variable or from a `_FILE` file outside git's working tree and checks not the string but what pgx makes of it: every address that pgx will try — the first one and the fallbacks — is either on this machine, the tests' loopback or a socket, or is reached over TLS that checks the server, that is, with `sslmode=verify-full`. A check of the string by parsing the URL could be bypassed: pgx reads the host from `?host=`, the last of two `sslmode` and `ssl=true` as `require`, and pgx's `require` without `sslrootcert` does not check the certificate (sources 3.5). A refusal names the variable, not the string.
- **The migrations** are goose, MIT, at the version of `go.mod`, by a provider without goose's global registry, on top of `database/sql` of the pgx driver:
  - the files `internal/store/migrations/NNNN_*.sql` are embedded in the binary (`//go:embed`), each goes in its own transaction, and the table `goose_db_version` holds the version;
  - forward only, without Down: `api` is rolled back only by rolling forward;
  - "add first, remove later": while a new revision rolls out, the old one still serves and reads the schema as it was. So `DROP`, `RENAME`, `TRUNCATE`, `ALTER … TYPE` and `SET NOT NULL` go only in a migration with the line `-- contract: <reason>`, where the reason is that nobody reads the old schema any more. A test holds this rule, the numbering from 0001 without gaps and the absence of Down;
  - the command `api migrate` writes to the log the event `migrated` — how many were applied and the schema version —, and a failure as the event `failure` with the moment `migrate`, by its kind. The deployment of `infra` runs it as a job of the new image, after `api check` and before the revision.
- **The schema of the account** is the migration `0001_accounts.sql`:
  - `accounts` — the UID of the sign-in service, the only unique field, which does not change; the owner's statement with its time; the PIN's hash, the counter, the start of the count and the lock; the tariff; the offset in minutes, from −720 to 840 and a multiple of 15; the end of the day; the date of the last request; the mark "being deleted". All the fields but the ID and the UID may be empty: after the mark, only the ID, the UID and the time of the mark stay in the row. The fields that a request updates are in no unique index: `FOR KEY SHARE` lets through only an `UPDATE` that touches no keys;
  - `invite_codes` — the SHA-256 of the code, the tariff, the time of redemption and the binding to an account. Redemption and binding are stored apart: deleting the account removes the binding, and the code stays redeemed;
  - `profiles` — the fields of the contract's `Profile`, the codes those of the contract; the owner profile is one per account and an adult, and an adult does not ask before showing;
  - `consents` — the adult's consent and the child consent, "waiting" without a profile or "confirmed";
  - `child_consent_records` — a minimal record without the ID of the account and of the profile;
  - the rows about a profile refer to it, and the profiles and the consents to the account, `ON DELETE CASCADE`: a row about a profile written after its deletion fails on the foreign key.

  The later migrations add the rest of the tables by the same rule.
- **A write under an account** is `store.WriteUnderAccount`: a transaction, the account's row `FOR KEY SHARE`, no row — "no account", a mark — "being deleted"; an error of the write rolls the transaction back. The other side is `store.MarkDeleting`, the first step of deletion: the row `FOR UPDATE`, then the mark, in the deletion's transaction. `FOR UPDATE` exactly: a plain `UPDATE` of the mark takes `FOR NO KEY UPDATE`, which does not conflict with `FOR KEY SHARE` and would let through a write that does not touch the account's row. A test holds both cases — a write that does not touch the account and a write that updates it: the mark waits for both, and the next write sees it.
- **The tests with the database** are `internal/store/storetest`:
  - the package's `TestMain` calls a helper, and it starts one `embedded-postgres` at the first test that needs a database, Postgres 17, the version the Neon projects are created with, on a free port; the migrations go once into a template database, and each test gets its own database — its copy, `CREATE DATABASE … TEMPLATE` — and can run in parallel;
  - the helper builds the supervisor with `go build` from the module's source code and passes `Config.Supervisor`: without a published version the library asks for it (sources 3.4);
  - `embedded-postgres` is a dependency of the tests, it does not enter the binary, and has no dependencies of its own (sources 3.5);
  - Codecov does not count the helper's lines: what it does not cover are failures of the environment, no `go` or Postgres did not start, and a test will not cause them.
- **The release's licenses** — `THIRD_PARTY` names pgx and goose with the modules they bring, each with its file `LICENSE` or `LICENSE.txt`. All are MIT, Apache-2.0 or BSD-3-Clause.

— *Why:*
- goose: OpenFGA requires it too, so it comes in any case, and the provider takes embedded files and holds the lock;
- the pooler for `serve`: Neon's direct connections go by the size of the compute, and the instances of Cloud Run grow with the load; a request of `api` keeps no session state;
- a server per package and a database per test: initdb and the start of Postgres take seconds, and a copy of the template milliseconds, and the tests' databases do not see each other.

— *Rejected:*
- tern: it works on pgx directly, but pulls in sprig and a dozen of its dependencies;
- a migrator of our own: code that would have to be maintained, when goose comes with OpenFGA anyway;
- golang-migrate: its module requires the drivers of a dozen databases and cloud SDKs;
- everything direct: the connections would hit Neon's limit as the instances grow;
- everything through the pooler, with goose's table lock: Neon names migrations work for a direct connection outright;
- Down migrations: `api`'s rollback is forward only, and a Down that nobody runs is not tested;
- only the rule and review: a violation would pass CI;
- a Postgres for each test: seconds per test.

— *Consequence:* `internal/store/`; `internal/store/migrations/`; `internal/store/storetest/`; `internal/config/`; `cmd/api/migrate.go`; `go.mod`; `justfile`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## Devcontainer

**R30. The `toolchain` stage of the devcontainer of `api` installs the package `libnss-wrapper` from a snapshot of the Ubuntu archive, and the helper of the database tests supplies through it a name for a uid that the image's `/etc/passwd` does not know. With the two volumes of the dev run, it is how the devcontainer of `api` differs from the container of `spec`, and a move to a new tag of `spec` compares `.devcontainer/` with the template and keeps the differences.**
- **The failure.** The shared `go.yml` of `.github` runs the `toolchain` stage under the runner's uid — `docker run --user "$(id -u):$(id -g)"` —, and the image has no such user. `initdb` looks up the name of its uid and stops: "could not look up effective user ID 1001: user does not exist". In the devcontainer the tests run as `vscode`, whom the image knows, and there is no failure.
- **The package and the helper:**
  - `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`, the stage `toolchain`, which is built `FROM toolchain-core`: `libnss-wrapper` from the snapshot of the Ubuntu archive `UBUNTU_SNAPSHOT`, which is raised by hand, like a version. apt reads a source of the snapshot alone: the `RUN` writes `snapshot.sources` — `URIs: https://snapshot.ubuntu.com/ubuntu/<UBUNTU_SNAPSHOT>/`, the suites and components of the base image's `ubuntu.sources`, `Signed-By: /usr/share/keyrings/ubuntu-archive-keyring.gpg` — into a folder of its own, gives `apt-get update` and `install` that folder alone, without `--snapshot`, and removes it with the lists, as the template of `spec` does for the kind `typescript`;
  - `toolchain-core`, which the release and the checks without a test build, holds nothing of `api`'s own and is the template's line for line; the comment of the stage `toolchain` names nss_wrapper in place of the template's words on `spec`'s tests, which would be wrong here;
  - `internal/store/storetest`, only when the uid is not in `/etc/passwd`: writes `passwd` and `group` with this uid into its temporary folder and sets `LD_PRELOAD`, `NSS_WRAPPER_PASSWD` and `NSS_WRAPPER_GROUP`; `embedded-postgres` passes the environment to the processes of Postgres. The production image has no such library: it is the distroless static image.
- **The differences from the container of `spec`.** Beyond the template of the kind `go` — the gopls step, its lock file and the Go extension —, there are three, and a move to a new tag keeps them in these places: nss_wrapper with its comment in the stage `toolchain`; the volume `novatrail-api-dev` of the dev configuration at `/home/vscode/.novatrail-dev`; and the volume `novatrail-dev-token` at `/home/vscode/.novatrail-token`, the custom token of the test account `dev-app` for the local build of `app`. `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json` mounts both volumes, and the Dockerfile makes their folders as `vscode` with the mode `700`. Go and its sums in the stage `toolchain` are the template's.

— *Why:*
- the database tests run in the same "Go / test" as the others, and their coverage goes to Codecov with the same upload; the fix does not wait for a release of another repository;
- nss_wrapper in the stage `toolchain` alone: it is for the tests of the database alone, and the release and "Go / lint", "Go / vuln", "Go / licenses" and "Documents" do not build or load it.

— *Rejected:*
- a change of `go.yml` of `.github` — mount the runner's `/etc/passwd` into the container: it would fix all the Go repositories, but the fix would wait for a release of `.github`, and the shared workflow is a change of another repository;
- an `/etc/passwd` open for writing in the `toolchain` stage, to which the helper appends its line: no package, but the devcontainer image becomes less strict;
- a CI job of its own for the database tests, and a skip in "Go / test": the database's coverage would leave the upload of "Go / coverage";
- nss_wrapper in the core: the release's build and four checks would carry an apt of the snapshot for no test, and `.github`'s workflows would read the core as `toolchain` and keep one stage;
- apt with `--snapshot`: it fetches the live archive's indexes too (measurements 3.2 spec).

— *Consequence:* `.devcontainer/Dockerfile`; `.devcontainer/devcontainer.json`; `internal/store/storetest/`.

## OpenFGA in the process

**R31. Rights are OpenFGA v1.22.1, as a library in the process of `api`: the package `pkg/server` on the Postgres storage in the database `openfga`, and the storage's schema is run by OpenFGA's own files through goose with a session lock, like the migrations of the database of `api`. The command `migrate` creates the store `api` and the embedded model of the rights, and `serve` only checks them. The port `internal/authz` checks `can_learn`, `can_manage` and `can_view`, writes and deletes the tuple `owner` and reads the account's tuples. The database `openfga` has connection strings of its own.**
- **The packages** are only `pkg/server`, `pkg/storage/postgres`, `pkg/storage/sqlcommon`, `pkg/logger` and `assets` of OpenFGA and `transformer` of `openfga/language` v0.3.2. The server is `server.NewServerWithOpts` with the storage `postgres.New`, and its methods — `Check`, `Write`, `Read`, `ListStores`, `CreateStore`, `WriteAuthorizationModel` — `api` calls directly, with the requests of `openfgav1`, without gRPC. `Close` closes the storage too (sources 2.4).
- **The storage's migrations are not `RunMigrations`.** Its package `pkg/storage/migrate` brings `go-sql-driver/mysql` under MPL-2.0 into the binary — outside the allowed list of licenses —, and it itself works on goose's global state and without a lock. The migration files of OpenFGA — `assets.EmbedMigrations`, `migrations/postgres` — are run by a goose provider with `WithSessionLocker` over a direct connection, as the migrations of the database of `api` are run. That the MySQL driver is not in the binary is held by `release-licenses`: the module is not in `THIRD_PARTY`, and `ci-lint` fails if it enters. The helper with the lock is one for both databases, `store.MigrateFiles`.
- **The store and the model.** Store names in OpenFGA are not unique, and a session lock cannot be taken through the pooler, so instances of `serve` starting together would create two stores or two models. Therefore:
  - `api migrate`, after the schema, under the session's `pg_advisory_lock` on the direct connection, finds or creates the store `api` and writes the embedded model only when no model of the store says the same. Models are compared without the ID, by the schema, the types and the conditions. Two stores `api` are a refusal, for both `migrate` and `serve`;
  - `serve` at start — `authz.Open` — finds the store and, among all its models, the one that says the same as the embedded one, and keeps to its ID. No store or no such model — it does not start: this means `migrate` did not run before the revision. Any such model is looked for, not the last one: while a new revision rolls out, its `migrate` has already written the new model, and the old revision still starts on cold instances — on its own; and a rollback to the old revision finds its model, and `migrate` does not write it a second time. So the model, like the schema, changes by the rule "add first, remove later": a new model does not remove what the old revision rests on;
  - the model is `internal/authz/model.fga`, word for word the block of the model in [architecture.md](architecture.md); a test holds one to the other.
- **The connection.** The storage's pool is its own, `store.Open`, checked by `Ping` under the start's context: a stop that was asked for while the database wakes up does not wait; the storage closes it along with itself.
- **The connection strings** are its own for the database `openfga`, as for the database `api`: `API_MIGRATE_OPENFGA_DATABASE_URL`, the direct one, for `migrate`, and `API_OPENFGA_DATABASE_URL`, the pooler's, for `serve`. Both are secrets of the configuration with the same check as the strings of the database `api`: every address is on this machine or over TLS that checks the server. The OpenFGA storage keeps no session state, and it works in Neon's pooler.
- **The port `authz.Rights`:**
  - `Can(account, right, profile)` is `Check` by the model found at opening, with `HIGHER_CONSISTENCY`. OpenFGA turns on no check caches by default, and there is no secondary database, so a check sees a write before it (sources 2.4). Another's profile and a profile that does not exist give the same "no";
  - `WriteOwner` and `DeleteOwner` — a repeat is harmless (`OnDuplicate` and `OnMissing` are `ignore`); `DeleteOwned` — in batches of 100, as many as OpenFGA takes; `OwnedProfiles` — all pages of `Read` by `user:<account ID>`, the relation `owner` and the type `learner`;
  - the model's objects are `user:<account ID>` and `learner:<profile ID>`.
- **"Before `COMMIT`".** OpenFGA has its own database, and there is no one transaction over both. The tuple is written from inside the open transaction of the database of `api` — in the `fn` of `store.WriteUnderAccount` —, after the profile's rows: if it was not written, the error of `fn` rolls the transaction back; if it was written and `COMMIT` failed, a "dangling" tuple without a profile row remains. It gives no rights to anything: a request by it finds no profile, and the daily job removes it. A test shows both outcomes.
- **The log.** OpenFGA's logger is noop: OpenFGA writes through zap, with the IDs of the store, the model and the request in fields that the log's guard would drop. `migrate` writes the event `migrated` for each database — the field `database`, `api` or `openfga`, and for `openfga` also `store_created` and `model_written`.
- **Cost.** OpenFGA brings 74 third-party modules into the binary — OpenFGA, grpc, otel, prometheus, cel-go, viper, zap and others, all under licenses of the list —, and `THIRD_PARTY` names each; five have a NOTICE, and grpc's is `NOTICE.txt`, and `release-licenses` carries it too. The size of the binary with it is measurements 1.1. The package `pkg/server` registers metrics in Prometheus's global registry at load; `api` does not serve them.

— *Why:*
- `migrate` already goes over the direct connection and once per deployment: under its lock the store and the model are not doubled, and `serve` has nothing to apply;
- strings of its own for `openfga` allow keeping the rights storage under a Neon role of its own or, in self-hosting, on another server;
- OpenFGA's migration files through a provider of our own give the same lock as the database of `api` has, and bring no MPL-2.0 license into the binary.

— *Rejected:*
- `RunMigrations`: MPL-2.0 in the binary, goose's global state and no lock at all;
- the store and the model at the start of `serve`, as MathTrail's canvas-api has them (sources 12.1): a race of instances and a spare store that would have to be cleaned up;
- an `openfga` string derived from the string of `api`: the rights storage would be tied to the same server and role;
- OpenFGA's log through the guard: its lines would become `unregistered_event` without fields;
- a check cache: a write of rights and a check after it would diverge for the cache's lifetime.

— *Consequence:* `internal/authz/`; `internal/store/`; `internal/config/`; `cmd/api/migrate.go`; `go.mod`; `justfile`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## The sign-in service port

**R32. The port of the sign-in service is `internal/authn`: the check of the ID token with revocation and the refusal class, deleting a user at the sign-in service and iterating over the users. The adapter is the Admin SDK for Go v4.22.0 whole, and the adapter's tests run on the real SDK against a stand-in Google over TLS, without the emulator. The service account's key is the secret `API_FIREBASE_CREDENTIALS`, only outside a deployment; in Cloud Run, the service's credentials, without a key.**
- **The port `authn.Port`** — the operations of the sign-in port:
  - `Verify` gives a `Token` — the UID and `auth_time` — or a `*Refusal` with a class: "expired", "invalid", "revoked", "no user", "disabled" or "failure". For "revoked" and "no user", the refusal has the UID too: `VerifyIDToken` reads it, by the signature, without a request. The SDK's errors come to the helpers `Is…` unwrapped, and the order matters: `IsIDTokenInvalid` is true for an expired token, for a revoked one and for the token of a disabled user alike;
  - `RemoveUser` is the step of deleting an account at the sign-in service: `RevokeRefreshTokens`, then `DeleteUser`, even if the revocation failed. "No user" means that the step is done already;
  - `Users` iterates over all users, with the time of the last sign-in and of the token's refresh, for the daily retention job;
  - `Fresh` and `FreshFor` are the fresh sign-in: `auth_time` not older than 300 s and not ahead of the clock of `api` by more than 300 s. The SDK does not check `auth_time` at all: it gives a tolerance of 300 s only to `iat` and `exp` (sources 2.5). So freshness is entirely the code of `api`.

  The port gives only the UID and the time. The code reads no email, name, phone or other claims, and a test holds this. The fake port for the tests of the handlers and the job is `authntest.Fake`.
- **Errors without data.** The texts of the SDK's errors name the UID — "no user exists with the uid: …" (sources 2.5). So an error of the port says only the class or the operation that failed, and the SDK's error is its segment. The log names this segment by its type, and it does not get into the answer to `app`.
- **The Admin SDK whole.** An `auth.Client` is built only through the root package `firebase.google.com/go/v4`: `auth.NewClient` takes an `internal.AuthConfig`, and an internal package cannot be imported from another module. The root package pulls in Firestore, Cloud Storage and Monitoring: 32 modules enter the binary, all under Apache-2.0, MIT or BSD-3 and without NOTICE (sources 2.5); the binary's growth is measurements 1.2.
- **The tests: a stand-in Google.** A `httptest` server with TLS answers for Google's keys for the ID token and for the calls of Identity Toolkit that the SDK makes: `accounts:lookup`, `accounts:update`, `accounts:delete` and `accounts:batchGet`. The test signs tokens with its own RS256 key, and the real SDK checks the signature, the expiry, the project and the revocation.
  - The SDK takes Google's keys with a client that cannot be passed from outside: it is a clone of `http.DefaultTransport` at the moment the client is built (sources 2.5). So the package's `TestMain` replaces `http.DefaultTransport`. The new transport connects only to the stand-in server and trusts only its certificate, so the test does not go out to the network. And if the SDK stops taking this transport, the tests fail rather than pass it by.
  - The calls of user management go through `option.WithHTTPClient`.
  - The devcontainer does not change.
- **The key** is a secret of the configuration: `API_FIREBASE_CREDENTIALS` or `API_FIREBASE_CREDENTIALS_FILE`.
  - A file in git's working tree is rejected, as for the connection strings.
  - Only a key of the type `service_account` is accepted. The adapter checks the type, because the library checks it only at the first call. The key is passed through `option.WithAuthCredentialsJSON` with the type: `WithCredentialsJSON` and `WithCredentialsFile` are deprecated "because of a potential security risk".
  - In a deployment, when `K_SERVICE` is set, the key is rejected, and outside a deployment it is required. Without a key, the adapter takes the service account's token only from the metadata server, `google.ComputeTokenSource`, with the scopes the SDK itself asks for. The deployment gives the service account its role. The adapter never takes the default credentials: they would read `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` too, and the file of `gcloud auth application-default login` — a user's credentials, perhaps of another project.
  - The project is `API_FIREBASE_PROJECT_ID`, and it is required: the Firebase project may differ from the Google Cloud project where `api` runs, and `api` does not let the SDK guess it from the environment.
  - With `FIREBASE_AUTH_EMULATOR_HOST` set, the adapter is not built: the SDK then does not check the token's signature and goes to the host of the variable over HTTP.
- **The class in the log and the time of a call.** The log names an error by the types of its segments, and all refusals have one type, `*authn.Refusal`. So the interceptor of the checks writes the class into the log line, as a separate field with the set of values of `Class`. The SDK repeats a call up to 4 times — on a network error and on 503 — and waits up to two minutes (sources 2.5), so the port's calls on a request's path go under the request's deadline.

— *Why:*
- a stand-in Google checks the same path as the deployment: the signature, the revocation and the UID of a revoked token without a second request. The emulator cannot do this: in its mode `VerifyIDToken` checks the revocation too (`c.isEmulator || checkRevokedOrDisabled`), so the UID of a revoked token and of the token of a deleted user cannot be read through it, and it does not check signatures (sources 2.5);
- a secret of its own keeps the key under the same check "outside the working tree" as the connection strings, and picks up no other credentials of the machine;
- "invalid" apart from "failure": a forged token is not a failure of the service, and with the class field of the request's log line, the log tells a forgery from an expired token;
- the SDK whole: the check of revocation and the parsing of the token stay the SDK's code.

— *Rejected:*
- the Firebase Auth emulator: Java 21 and `firebase-tools` in the devcontainer and CI, tokens without a signature, and the path of the UID of a revoked token, which it would not check;
- a stand-in emulator through `FIREBASE_AUTH_EMULATOR_HOST`: the same trouble with the UID of a revoked token, and the signature is not checked;
- `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS` and the default credentials in general: the path to the key goes past the check of the working tree, and the machine's credentials, gcloud's of another project included, would be picked up by themselves;
- a user's credentials through `gcloud auth application-default login`: gcloud is needed in the devcontainer, which is a difference from the container of `spec`, and Identity Toolkit requires a quota project with them;
- a JWT check of our own and REST calls without the SDK: the binary would be 18 MB smaller, but `api` would hold a copy of the SDK's check of the token;
- "invalid" as "failure": a forgery would get `unavailable`, and `app` would repeat the request.

— *Consequence:* `internal/authn/`; `internal/authn/authntest/`; `internal/config/`; `go.mod`; `justfile`; `docs/architecture.md`.

## The Connect server and the checks

**R33. `serve` answers by the contract `novatrail.api.v1` on Connect v2 — `connectrpc.com/connect/v2` v2.0.2, on which the contracts module is built —, and before the handler each request passes the checks by an interceptor, by the table of methods: the token class, the mark "being deleted", the account, the right to the profile of its message and fresh sign-in last. The request's deadline is 30 s, the largest message 11 MiB, the date of the last request is written once a day, and a user deleted bypassing `api` has their account deleted. CORS only to the exact origin of `app` from `API_APP_ORIGIN`.**
- **The server.** `apiv1connect.Register<Service>Handler` registers all the services of the contract on the `connect.Server`, and `connecthttp.Mount` puts their methods on the same mux as `/health`. A handler that does not exist yet is `Unimplemented<Service>Handler`: the method answers `unimplemented`, but after its checks. The codecs are binary protobuf and JSON with proto names (`UseProtoNames`); Connect itself drops unknown JSON fields.
- **The largest message** of a request is `rpc.MaxMessage`, 11 MiB, through `connecthttp.WithReadMaxBytes`; more is `resource_exhausted` before parsing. The largest message of the contract is the scan of a consent form, up to 10 MiB.
